Six-loop beta functions in general scalar theory
A. Bednyakov, and A. Pikelner

Abstract
We consider general renormalizable scalar field theory and derive six-loop beta functions for all parameters in d = 4 d=4 dimensions within the MS ¯ \overline{\mathrm{MS}} -scheme. We do not explicitly compute relevant loop integrals but utilize O ⁡ ( n ) O(n) -symmetric model counter-terms available in the literature. We consider dimensionless couplings and parameters with a mass scale, ranging from the trilinear self-coupling to the vacuum energy. We use obtained results to extend renormalization-group equations for several vector, matrix, and tensor models to the six-loop order. Also, we apply our general expressions to derive new contributions to beta functions and anomalous dimensions in the scalar sector of the Two-Higgs-Doublet Model.



1
Introduction

The renormalization group (RG) plays an essential role in high-energy physics
and the theory of critical phenomena. In particle physics, one can use RG to
re-sum specific radiative corrections making theory predictions valid in a wide
range of energy scales. In the study of critical phenomena, the RG approach
allows one to study phase transitions and predict critical exponents of the
second-order transitions with high accuracy.

A convenient tool to compute the RG functions that drive the dependence of model
parameters on the scale is to use a perturbative expansion of dimensional
regularized theory
tHooft:1973mfk
together with modified minimal
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
subtraction of
infinities. The latter appear in loop integrals and manifest themselves in
d

dimensions as poles in
\varepsilon=(4-d)/2
. One cancels the poles by a finite set of
renormalization constants.

There is significant progress in the calculation of beta functions and anomalous
dimensions in the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme. At the two-loop level, the RG functions are known
in any general renormalizable quantum field theory (QFT) in
d=4

dimensions
Machacek:1983fi
;
Machacek:1983tz
;
Machacek:1984zw
;
Luo:2002ey
;
Schienbein:2018fsw
.
Despite several calculations of three-loop (and even four-loop) RG functions in
particular particle-physics
models
Mihaila:2012fm
;
Bednyakov:2012rb
;
Bednyakov:2012en
;
Chetyrkin:2013wya
;
Bednyakov:2013eba
;
Herren:2017uxn
;
Bednyakov:2018cmx
;
Bednyakov:2015ooa
;
Zoller:2015tha
;
Chetyrkin:2016ruf
;
Davies:2019onf
,
general three-loop results are not yet available. Recently, an essential step
has been made in this
direction
Poole:2019kcm
;
Steudtner:2020tzo
;
Steudtner:2021fzs
;
Thomsen:2021ncy
.
The main idea is to enumerate all possible “tensor” structures that can appear
in the RG functions at a certain loop level and compute the corresponding
unknown coefficients by matching them to specific models. Not long ago, this
approach allowed authors of the paper
Steudtner:2020tzo
to derive general
three-loop RG functions in a pure scalar model.

Our paper does not follow this strategy and extends the results for general
scalar theories up to six loops by more conventional technique, i.e., by
computing contributions from individual Feynman graphs. Such a leap in the loop
level is due to the significant progress in calculating critical exponents in
scalar theories. Thanks to the authors
of ref.
Kompaniets:2017yct
, the required renormalization constants can be
found given the diagram-by-diagram results of the
\mathrm{KR}^{\prime}
operation.
Application of the latter to a Feynman graph produces the corresponding
counter-term in the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme.

We consider the following general renormalizable Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{a}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{a}-\frac{m^{2}_{ab}}{2}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}-\frac{h_{abc}}{3!}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\phi_{c}-\frac{\lambda_{abcd}}{4!}\phi_{a}\phi_{b}\phi_{c}\phi_{d}-t_{a}\phi_{a}-\Lambda

(1)

for real scalar fields
\phi_{a}
. The mass parameters
m_{ab}^{2}
, cubic
h_{abc}
and quartic couplings
\lambda_{abcd}
are symmetric in their indices. For completeness we also add the tadpole term proportional to
t_{a}
, and the vacuum energy term
\Lambda
.

Here we present the six-loop RG equations in the MS-scheme for the field

\phi_{a}
and all parameters of eq. (
1
).
The RG function for a parameter
A=\{\lambda_{abcd},h_{abc},m^{2}_{ab},t_{a},\Lambda\}
is defined as

\displaystyle\beta_{A}

\displaystyle\equiv\mu\frac{\partial A}{\partial\mu}=\sum_{l}h^{l}\beta_{A}^{(l)},\qquad h=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}},

(2)

where
\beta_{A}^{(l)}
corresponds to the
l
-loop contribution.
The field anomalous dimension is given by

\displaystyle\phi_{a,0}

\displaystyle=Z_{ab}\phi_{b}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\phi}_{ab}=Z_{ac}^{-1}\cdot\mu\frac{\partial Z_{cb}}{\partial\mu}=-\mu\frac{\partial Z^{-1}_{ac}}{\partial\mu}\cdot Z_{cb}

(3)

and is related to the field renormalization constant
Z_{ab}
.
It is worth pointing that the latter can be multiplied by an arbitrary orthogonal matrix without spoiling divergence cancellation in two-point functions. Due to this, the antisymmetric part of
\gamma^{\phi}_{ab}
is not fixed and leads to ambiguities
1
1

1

We are grateful to F. Herren for bringing our attention to this fact.
in the RG functions.
Nevertheless, the ambiguity is related to the freedom in the basis choice and does not affect physical observables (see discussions in refs.
Bednyakov:2014pia
;
Herren:2017uxn
). In what follows we use symmetric
\gamma^{\phi}_{ab}
.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
contains details of
our calculation. In section
3
we apply our general results
to the cases known in the literature. In particular, we consider
vector (section
3.1
), matrix (section
3.2
), and tensor
(section
3.3
) models possessing different kinds of symmetries.
Also, we extend known three-loop results for the Two-Higgs-Doublet Model (2HDM)
to six loops in section
3.4
. Section
4
contains a
discussion of the results and conclusions. In
appendix
A
we provide a derivation of the RG
functions for dimensionful couplings in a general form.

2
Details of calculation

As the calculation method, we decided to use an approach similar to the one
in ref.
Kompaniets:2017yct
, based on the direct computation of the necessary
counter-terms from individual diagrams. However, in our work, we avoid the
calculation of any loop integrals. The authors of
ref.
Kompaniets:2017yct
considered all the required six-loop graphs in
the context of the
O(n)
-symmetric model
2
2

2

Seven-loop RG functions for the
O(n)
model are also known due to O. Schnetz
Schnetz:2016fhy
.
and made the corresponding counter
terms available in a computer-readable form. One can adopt the latter for more
complicated theories by changing model-dependent prefactors. In this way,
six-loop renormalization-group functions for
O(n)
theory with cubic
anisotropy
Adzhemyan:2019gvv
and
O(n)\times O(m)
symmetric
model
Kompaniets:2019xez
were derived.

To perform calculations with general Lagrangian (
1
), we
prepare a
DIANA

Tentyukov:1999is
model file. We use special
mapping rules between its internal topology format and diagram topologies, which
are identified in ref.
Kompaniets:2017yct
and given in the Nickel index
notation. After generating all needed two- and four-point functions with

DIANA
and performing all needed index contractions with

FORM

Vermaseren:1992vn
, we substitute actual values for momentum
integrals by counter-terms from the available tables
Kompaniets:2017yct
.
It is trivial to extract the RG functions
\gamma^{\phi}_{ab}
and
\beta_{abcd}\equiv\beta_{\lambda_{abcd}}
from the first
\varepsilon
pole in the sum of counter-terms.

The obtained results involve a certain number of tensor structures, i.e.,
products of (up to 12) general couplings
\lambda_{abcd}
with all but four
(two) indices contracted in
\beta_{abcd}
(
\gamma^{\phi}_{ab}
). We can simplify
corresponding expressions by identifying tensor structures identical up to
the renaming of contracted indices. Also, since the corresponding numeric
coefficient depends only on the Feynman graph, we collect all the structures,
which are different only by permutations of external indices
abcd
. As a
consequence, we can cast our main result for
\beta_{abcd}
into the form

\beta_{abcd}=\sum\limits_{l=1}^{6}h^{l}\sum\limits_{i}^{n_{l}}T_{i,abcd}^{(l)}C_{i}^{(l)},

(4)

where
n_{l}=\{1,2,7,23,110,571\}
is the number of unique tensor structures

T_{i,abcd}^{(l)}
at
l
loops. The coefficients
C_{i}^{(l)}
are pure
numbers. To deduce the expressions for
T_{i,abcd}^{(l)}
, we made use of Nickel
index notation
nickel1977compilation
for graph representation of tensor
contractions and utilized the
GraphState

package
Batkovich:2014bla
. As an example, we give here one of the
three-loop structures

T^{(3)}_{4,abcd}\equiv\frac{1}{4!}\left[\lambda_{ai_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}\lambda_{bi_{3}i_{4}i_{6}}\lambda_{ci_{2}i_{4}i_{5}}\lambda_{di_{1}i_{5}i_{6}}+\mathrm{perm.}\right]=\vbox{\hbox{

(5)

where we indicate the corresponding Nickel index and emphasize the normalization of
T^{(l)}_{i,abcd}
together with
the fact that the latter are symmetric in
abcd
.

We provide a table containing a minimal set of unique tensor structures formed
by different contractions between
\lambda_{abcd}
indices and the corresponding
coefficients
3
3

3

Examination of the structures contributing to
\gamma^{\phi}_{ab}
leads us to the conclusion that in pure scalar theories the ambiguity in RG functions can manifests itself starting from five loops.
. Given these tables, we derive the beta functions for dimensionful
parameters entering (
1
) employing the so-called dummy field
method
Martin:1993zk
;
Luo:2002ti
;
Schienbein:2018fsw
. The core of the
technique is to introduce “dummy” non-propagating field(s)
x_{a}
, e.g., by
shifting all (or just one) components of the vector
\phi_{a}\to\phi_{a}+x_{a}
.
Contracting
\beta_{abcd}
with one or more dummy fields
x_{a}
, we can readily
obtain the expressions for
\beta_{\Lambda}
,
\beta_{a}\equiv\beta_{t_{a}}
,

\beta_{ab}\equiv\beta_{m^{2}_{ab}}
, and
\beta_{abc}\equiv\beta_{h_{abc}}

(see appendix
A
). Indeed, we consider
4
4

4

We use
compact notation
\beta_{xxxx}\equiv\beta_{abcd}x_{a}x_{b}x_{c}x_{d}
, etc.

\beta_{xxxx}
,

\beta_{axxx}
,
\beta_{abxx}
, together with
\beta_{abcx}
, and identify
\lambda_{abcx}\equiv h_{abc}
,
\lambda_{abxx}\equiv 2m^{2}_{ab}
,
\lambda_{axxx}\equiv 3!t_{a}
,
\lambda_{xxxx}\equiv 4!\Lambda
.
The only subtlety here is that we have to remove contributions from external leg
renormalization, leading to tadpole diagrams in the final answer (see ref.
Schienbein:2018fsw

for details). We can immediately identify corresponding tensor structures in
general expression for
\beta_{abcd}

}}=0,

(6)

where dotted lines represent
dummy field
x
. We use tilde to denote the quantities with tadpole contribution removed, and write

\displaystyle\beta_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}\cdot\tilde{\beta}_{xxxx},

\displaystyle\beta_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}\cdot\tilde{\beta}_{axxx},

\displaystyle\beta_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\tilde{\beta}_{abxx},

\displaystyle\beta_{abc}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{abcx}.

(7)

The tensor structures, including the corresponding graphs and coefficients for all the considered RG functions, can be found in the form of supplementary
Mathematica
files.

3
From general results to specific models

In this section we demonstrate the application of our general results to particular scalar models. It is worth mentioning that we heavily rely on
FORM

Vermaseren:1992vn
to deal with index contractions and algebraic simplifications in the case of matrix fields.

3.1
Warming up with
O(n)
-symmetric model

Our first example is the well-known
O(n)
symmetric model, which has a long
history in the study of critical phenomena (see ref.
Kompaniets:2017yct

and reference therein).
The following Euclidean Lagrangian describes the theory

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\vec{\phi}(-\partial^{2}+m^{2})\vec{\phi}+\frac{\lambda}{4!}(\vec{\phi}\cdot\vec{\phi})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\phi\phi}d_{ab}\phi_{a}\phi_{b},

(8)

where
\vec{\phi}=\{\phi_{a}\}
,
a=1,...,n
is a
n
-component scalar field. We also add a quadratic operator involving traceless symmetric tensor
d_{ab}
multiplied by a source
g_{\phi\phi}
. The anomalous dimension
5
5

5

In ref.
Kompaniets:2019zes
the notation
\gamma_{\mathcal{\tilde{E}}}=\gamma_{\phi\phi}
is used.

\gamma_{\phi\phi}
of the corresponding operator is related to the so-called crossover exponent (see, e.g., refs.
Kirkham:1981pu
;
Kompaniets:2019zes
) and can be found in our approach as

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi\phi}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{g_{\phi\phi}}+2\gamma_{\phi}

(9)

with
\beta_{g_{\phi\phi}}
being the beta function of
g_{\phi\phi}
and
\gamma_{\phi}
corresponding to the anomalous dimension of the field computed via eq. (
3
).
This and other RG functions can be easily obtained from our general result by means of substitutions

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{3}\left(\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}+\delta_{ac}\delta_{bd}+\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}\right),

(10)

\displaystyle m^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle=m^{2}\delta_{ab}+g_{\phi\phi}d_{ab}.

(11)

In our calculation we find perfect agreement with previous computations.
Our new result is related to the six-loop contribution to the beta function of the vacuum energy
\beta_{\Lambda}
for
g_{\phi\phi}=0
(see refs.
Kastening:1997ah
;
Larin:1997ek
for the five-loop expression).
Using the notation
g\equiv h\lambda
(c.f. ref.
Kastening:1997ah
) we have
6
6

6

In ref.
Kastening:1997ah
RG functions are defined as derivatives w.r.t
\ln\mu^{2}=2\ln\mu
.
The factor
1\over 2
in (
12
) is introduced for convenience.

\displaystyle\beta_{v}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{16\pi^{2}}{m^{4}}\beta_{\Lambda}=\frac{n}{4}+\frac{n(n+2)}{96}g^{2}+\frac{n(n+2)g^{3}}{1296}(n+8)(12\zeta_{3}-25)

\displaystyle+\frac{n(n+2)g^{4}}{82944}\Big[16\zeta_{3}(3n^{2}-382n-1700)+96\zeta_{4}(4n^{2}+39n+146)

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt-1024\zeta_{5}(5n+22)-319n^{2}+13968n+64864\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{n(n+2)g^{5}}{1866240}\Big[384\zeta_{3}^{2}(41n^{2}-206n-888)-960\zeta_{6}(70n^{2}+809n+2118)

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt+96\zeta_{5}(45n^{3}+890n^{2}+19348n+67440)-288\zeta_{4}(n^{3}+294n^{2}+3088n+9496)

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt-48\zeta_{3}(51n^{3}+700n^{2}-2964n+2024)-1419n^{3}-17124n^{2}-2166136n-7308224

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt+576\zeta_{7}(67n^{2}+1405n+4306)+1152\zeta_{3}\zeta_{4}(2n^{2}+145n+582)\Big].

(12)

By simple rescaling
\lambda\to 3!\lambda
, one can easily get the six-loop contributions to the RG functions for the Standard Model Higgs potential parameters (including the vacuum energy) from the results of
O(4)
theory.

3.2
Matrix models

We consider matrix models with real and complex fields described by the following Lagrangians

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\phi(-\partial^{2}+\tau)\phi^{T}\right]+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{4!}(\mathrm{Tr}\left[\phi\phi^{T}\right])^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4!}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\phi\phi^{T}\phi\phi^{T}\right]

(13)

for real
\phi
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{Tr}[\phi(-\partial^{2}+\tau)\phi^{\dagger}]+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{4!}(\mathrm{Tr}[\phi\phi^{\dagger}])^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4!}\mathrm{Tr}[\phi\phi^{\dagger}\phi\phi^{\dagger}]

(14)

for complex
\phi
.
To deal with matrix models we make use of the following decomposition (see also ref.
Litim:2020jvl
)

\displaystyle\phi=\sum\limits_{a=1}^{N_{a}}\chi_{a}T_{a},

(15)

where
\chi_{a}
are real fields, and there are
N_{a}
independent matrices
T_{a}
, which encode all the degrees of freedom present in
\phi
. Substituting (
15
) into either (
13
) or (
14
), we can rewrite the Lagrangians in the form (
1
).
One can see that we completely get rid of the initial matrix indices of
\phi
and replace them with a single one
a=1,\ldots,N_{a}
.
Given eqs. (
13
) and (
14
), for the fields
\chi^{a}
to be canonically normalized, we have to ensure that

\left[(T^{a})^{\dagger}\equiv\bar{T}^{a}\right]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}T_{b}^{T})+\mathrm{Tr}(T_{b}T_{a}^{T})

\displaystyle=2\delta_{ab},

\displaystyle\text{for real }\phi,

(16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}\bar{T}_{b})+\mathrm{Tr}(T_{b}\bar{T}_{a})

\displaystyle=\delta_{ab},

\displaystyle\text{for complex }\phi.

(17)

As a consequence, one can identify

\displaystyle m^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\tau\delta_{ab},

(18)

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{4!}\left[T^{ab}T^{cd}+\text{perm.}\right]+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4!}\left[T^{abcd}+\text{perm.}\right],

(19)

where

\displaystyle T^{ab}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}T_{b}^{T})\equiv T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}},

\displaystyle T^{abcd}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}T_{b}^{T}T_{c}T_{d}^{T})\equiv T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}},

\displaystyle\text{for real }\phi,

(20)

\displaystyle T^{ab}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}\bar{T}_{b})\equiv T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}},

\displaystyle T^{abcd}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}\bar{T}_{b}T_{c}\bar{T}_{d})\equiv T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}},

\displaystyle\text{for complex }\phi.

(21)

In eq. (
19
) all 24 permutations of the indices
abcd
are taken into account. Obviously, the number of terms can be reduced in specific models.
In the following subsections we provide some details of our calculations for the cases discussed in the literature.

3.2.1
Real anti-symmetric field

The Lagrangian of the model is given by eq. (
13
) with

\phi
being an antisymmetric
n\times n
matrix,
\phi^{T}=-\phi
. The
model was considered in refs.
Antonov_2013
;
Antonov:2017pqv
and the four-loop results can be found in ref.
lebedev2018critical
.

To use our general formulae, we utilize the decomposition (
15
) with
N_{a}=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
and
T_{a}=t_{a}
corresponding to antisymmetric generators of
SO(n)
. The latter satisfy

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(t^{a}t^{b})=T_{f}\delta_{ab},\quad t^{a}_{ij}t^{a}_{kl}=\frac{T_{f}}{2}\left(\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\right).

(22)

To keep the standard normalization for the fields
\chi_{a}
, we use
T_{f}=1
(see eq. (
16
)).
The number of terms in eq. (
19
) can be reduced (
\mathrm{Tr}t^{a}t^{b}\ldots\equiv T^{ab\ldots}
)

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{3}\left[T^{ab}T^{cd}+T^{ac}T^{bd}+T^{ad}T^{bc}\right]+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{3}\left[T^{abcd}+T^{abdc}+T^{acbd}\right],

(23)

where we used the cyclic symmetry of the trace operation and the fact that
t_{a}^{T}=-t_{a}
.

By means of eq. (
22
) we write down the rules, which allow one to simplify the products of traces involving
t_{a}
with some of the indices contracted.
Substituting (
23
) into the general expression for
\beta_{abcd}
, and performing the above-mentioned algebraic simplifications, we obtain

\beta_{abcd}
of the form

\displaystyle 4!\beta_{abcd}

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n)\left[T^{ab}T^{cd}+\text{perm.}\right]+f_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n)\left[T^{abcd}+\text{perm.}\right],

(24)

where
f_{1,2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n)
are some polynomials of their arguments. It is possible to extract
the beta functions for
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
from eq. (
24
) by applying suitable projectors.
However, one can also use the fact that by construction
\beta_{abcd}
is symmetric in all the indices. Setting the latter equal to each other in the end of calculation, we have

\displaystyle\beta_{aaaa}

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n)[T^{aa}]^{2}+f_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n)[T^{aaaa}],\quad\text{no sum over }a.

(25)

Comparing eqs. (
25
) and
(
23
) with
b=c=d=a
, one can easily deduce that

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n),\quad\beta_{\lambda_{2}}=f_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},n).

(26)

We utilize this approach to obtain relevant RG functions up to the six-loop level.
Our results agree with that given in refs.
Antonov:2017pqv
;
lebedev2018critical

7
7

7

Note that in ref.
Antonov:2017pqv
the notation
\lambda_{i}=g_{i}
is used and the RG functions are expanded in
g_{i}/(8\pi^{2})
.
.
It is worth noting that for
n=2
and
n=3
the model is equivalent to one-component
\phi^{4}
and the
O(3)
-vector theory considered in sec.
3.1
, respectively.
Indeed, combining
\lambda=\lambda_{1}+{1\over 2}\lambda_{2}
and computing
\beta_{\lambda}=\beta_{\lambda_{1}}+{1\over 2}\beta_{\lambda_{2}}
for
n=2
and
n=3
we get the expected results.

Full six-loop beta functions and anomalous dimensions are available online as supplementary material.
For convenience, we present here our expressions for the one-loop

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{6}(n^{2}-n+16)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(2n-1)+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{2},

(27)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{6}(2n-1),

(28)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv-\beta^{(1)}_{\tau}/\tau=-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{6}(n^{2}-n+4)-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{6}(2n-1),

(29)

and two-loop RG functions

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{288}\left[4\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}\right](n^{2}-n+4)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{36}(2n-1),

(30)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}}{6}(3n-3n^{2}-28)-\left[\frac{11\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{12}\right](2n-1)-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda^{2}_{2}}{72}(5n^{2}-5n+164),

(31)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{18}(5n-5n^{2}-164)-\frac{11\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9}(2n-1)+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{24}(n-n^{2}-20),

(32)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{144}\left[4\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}\right](n^{2}-n+4)+\frac{5\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{18}(2n-1).

(33)

3.2.2
O(n)\times O(m)
model

Let us now consider a matrix model, which is invariant under
O(n)\times O(m)
group.
It describes the critical thermodynamics of frustrated spin systems with noncollinear and noncoplanar ordering (see, e.g., ref.
Kompaniets:2019xez
and references therein). In refs.
Calabrese:2003ww
five-loop results are presented in terms of
u=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}
, and
v=\lambda_{2}
. Six-loop RG functions are also known
Kompaniets:2019xez
in terms of
g_{i}=\lambda_{i}
.

The Landau-Wilson Lagrangian can be written in the form (
13
) with

\phi=\{\phi_{\alpha i}\}
being
n\times m
real matrix field, and
\alpha=1,\ldots,n
,
i=1,\ldots,m
.

To compute relevant RG functions from our general result we interpret
\chi_{a}
in eq. (
15
) as
N_{a}=n\cdot m
matrix elements of
\phi
, so that
each of
n\times m
real matrices
T^{a}
has only one non-zero element

\displaystyle(T_{a})_{\alpha i}=\sqrt{T_{f}}\cdot\delta_{\alpha,((a-1)~\text{div}~m)+1}\delta_{i,((a-1)~\text{mod}~m)+1},

(34)

where we introduce
T_{f}=1
for convenience. As a consequence
8
8

8

Notice here that
T_{a}T_{b}^{T}
are
n\times n
matrices, while
T_{a}^{T}T_{b}
have
m\times m
dimension.
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}T^{T}_{b})=\mathrm{Tr}(T^{T}_{b}T_{a})=T_{f}\delta_{ab},\quad T_{\alpha i}^{a}T_{\beta j}^{a}=T_{f}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{ij}.

(35)

The quartic self-coupling is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{3}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}T^{b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}T^{b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{6}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}d{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}d{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}\right],

(36)

where to reduce the number of terms in LHS, we use the fact that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(A)=\mathrm{Tr}(A^{T}),\quad(T_{a}T^{T}_{b}T_{c}T^{T}_{d})^{T}=T_{d}T^{T}_{c}T_{b}T^{T}_{a}

so

\displaystyle T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}\equiv\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}T^{T}_{b}T_{c}T^{T}_{d})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(T_{d}T^{T}_{c}T_{b}T^{T}_{a})\equiv T^{d{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a}}.

(37)

To extract the RG functions, we substitute (
36
) together with (
18
) into
\beta_{abcd}
,
\beta_{ab}
and
\gamma_{ab}
and use the rules (
35
) to simplify the products of traces involving
T_{a}
and
T_{b}^{T}
.

We use known results
Calabrese:2003ww
;
Kompaniets:2019xez
to cross-check our expressions, which at the one-loop order are given by

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{3}(8+nm)+\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(1+n+m)+\lambda_{2}^{2},

(38)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{3}(4+m+n),

(39)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv-\beta^{(1)}_{\tau}/\tau=-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{3}(2+nm)-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{3}(1+m+n),

(40)

while at two loops we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{36}(2+mn)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{18}(1+m+n)+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{2}}{72}(3+mn+m+n),

(41)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}}{3}(14+3mn)-\frac{22\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}(1+m+n)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{18}(87+5(mn+m+n))-\frac{\lambda^{2}_{3}}{3}(3+m+n),

(42)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}(82+5mn)-\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9}(11(n+m)+29)-\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{6}(17+mn+3(m+n)),

(43)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{5\lambda_{1}^{2}}{18}(2+mn)+\frac{5\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{9}(1+m+n)+\frac{5\lambda^{2}_{2}}{36}(3+mn+m+n).

(44)

In addition, we extend to the six-loop order the anomalous dimensions of quadratic operators considered in refs.
DePrato:2006jx
;
Pelissetto:2007gw
:

\displaystyle Q^{(1)}_{\alpha i\beta j}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\alpha i}\phi_{\beta j}-\phi_{\alpha j}\phi_{\beta i},

(45)

\displaystyle Q^{(2)}_{\alpha i\beta j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\phi_{\alpha i}\phi_{\beta j}+\phi_{\alpha j}\phi_{\beta i}\right)-\frac{1}{n}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\phi_{\delta i}\phi_{\delta j}-\frac{1}{m}\delta_{ij}\phi_{\alpha k}\phi_{\beta k}+\frac{1}{nm}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{ij}\phi_{\delta k}\phi_{\delta k},

(46)

\displaystyle Q^{(3)}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\delta i}\phi_{\delta j}-\frac{1}{m}\delta_{ij}\phi_{\delta k}\phi_{\delta k}\equiv\tilde{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta ij}\delta_{\alpha\beta},

(47)

\displaystyle Q^{(4)}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\alpha k}\phi_{\beta k}-\frac{1}{n}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\phi_{\delta k}\phi_{\delta k}\equiv\tilde{Q}^{(4)}_{\alpha\beta ij}\delta_{ij}

(48)

and belonging to different representations of
O(n)\times O(m)
.
The operators can be
treated in our approach in a similar fashion.
We assume that the perturbations can be added to the Lagrangian with the corresponding sources (“masses”) and rewritten in terms of
\chi
-fields as, e.g.,

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{m}^{2}_{1,\alpha i\beta j}\right]\left[Q^{(1)}_{\alpha i\beta j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\tilde{m}^{2}_{1,cd}T^{c}_{\alpha i}T^{d}_{\beta j}\right]\left[\chi^{a}\chi^{b}\left(T^{a}_{\alpha i}T^{b}_{\beta j}-T^{a}_{\alpha j}T^{b}_{\beta j}\right)\right]\equiv m^{2}_{1,ab}\chi^{a}\chi^{b}

(49)

\displaystyle m^{2}_{1,ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{m}^{2}_{1,cd}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}T^{b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}-T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}+(a\leftrightarrow b)\right].

(50)

Since the operators (
48
) do not mix under renormalization, we use the following substitutions
9
9

9

Given eq. (
37
), one can prove that tensors multiplying
\tilde{m}^{2}_{i,cd}
are symmetric in
a\leftrightarrow b
and
c\leftrightarrow d
.

\displaystyle Q^{(1)}_{\alpha i\beta j}:\quad m^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\tilde{m}^{2}_{1,cd}}{2}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}T^{b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}-T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}+(a\leftrightarrow b)\right],

(51)

\displaystyle Q^{(2)}_{\alpha i\beta j}:\quad m^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\tilde{m}^{2}_{2,cd}}{4}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}T^{b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}b{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}}-\frac{2}{n}T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}d{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}\right.

\displaystyle\phantom{\Rightarrow\frac{\tilde{m}^{2}_{2,cd}}{4}}\left.-\frac{2}{m}T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+(a\leftrightarrow b)\right]+\frac{\tilde{m}^{2}_{2,cd}}{nm}T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}},

(52)

\displaystyle Q^{(3)}_{ij}:\quad m^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\tilde{m}^{2}_{3,cd}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}d{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}+(a\leftrightarrow b)\right]-\frac{1}{m}T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}\right],

(53)

\displaystyle Q^{(4)}_{\alpha\beta}:\quad m^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\tilde{m}^{2}_{4,cd}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}+(a\leftrightarrow b)\right]-\frac{1}{n}T^{a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}\right]

(54)

and extract the beta functions
(\beta_{\tilde{m}^{2}_{i}})_{cd}\equiv-\tilde{\gamma}_{i}\cdot\tilde{m}^{2}_{i,cd}
of
\tilde{m}^{2}_{i,cd}
,
i=1,4

from the corresponding terms in
\beta_{ab}
. The RG functions for the operators
Q^{(i)}
(
48
) are obtained by adding the contribution from the field anomalous dimension
\gamma_{\chi}=\gamma_{\phi}
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{Q_{i}}=\tilde{\gamma}_{i}{+}2\gamma_{\phi}.

(55)

A welcome check of the result is the fact that for
v=0
all
\gamma_{Q_{i}}
coincide. We also compare our expressions with that given in ref.
Pelissetto:2007gw
and find perfect agreement up to five loops
10
10

10

The results of ref.
Pelissetto:2007gw
are written in terms of
(u,v)/(8\pi^{2})
.
.
We present here our one-loop,

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{Q_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{3},

(56)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{Q_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{3},

(57)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{Q_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\lambda_{1}+(1+n)\lambda_{2}}{3},

(58)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{Q_{4}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\lambda_{1}+(1+m)\lambda_{2}}{3},

(59)

and two-loop results

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{Q_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{9}(6+mn)+\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{9}(1+m+n)+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{2}}{18}(1-m-n),

(60)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{Q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{9}(6+mn)+\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{9}(3+m+n)+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{2}}{18}(9+m+n),

(61)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{Q_{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{9}(6+mn)+\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{9}(3+m+3n)+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{2}}{18}(9+mn+m+3n),

(62)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{Q_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{9}(6+mn)+\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{9}(3+3m+n)+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{2}}{18}(9+mn+3m+n).

(63)

The six-loop expressions are available as supplementary material.

3.2.3
Complex anti-symmetric field

Let us now generalize the model discussed in sec.
3.2.1
and consider complex antisymmetric
n\times n
matrices
\phi
. The corresponding Lagrangian (
14
) can be used to study phase transitions in quantum Fermi systems within the RG approach (see ref.
komarova2013temperature
). We decompose the field via (
15
) with
N_{a}=n(n-1)
and antisymmetric

\displaystyle T^{a}=\begin{cases}t^{a}&a=1,\ldots,n(n-1)/2,\\
it^{a}&a=1+n(n-1)/2,\ldots,n(n-1).\end{cases}

The latter are written in terms of generators
t^{a}
of
SO(n)
. Given
\mathrm{Tr}(t^{a}t^{b})=T_{f}\delta_{ab}
, one can derive
\left[(T^{a})^{\dagger}\equiv\bar{T}^{a}\right]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T^{a}\bar{T}^{b})+\mathrm{Tr}(T^{b}\bar{T}^{a})=-2T_{f}\delta_{ab},\quad T^{a}_{ij}T^{a}_{kl}=\sum\limits_{b=1}^{N_{a}/2}\left(t^{b}_{ij}t^{b}_{ij}+i^{2}t^{b}_{ij}t^{b}_{ij}\right)=0,

\displaystyle T^{a}_{ij}\bar{T}^{a}_{kl}=\sum\limits_{b}^{N_{a}/2}\left(t_{ij}^{b}(-t^{b}_{kl})+it_{ij}^{b}(+it^{b}_{kl})\right)=-T_{f}\left(\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\right).

(64)

One can see from eq. (
17
) that for
T_{f}=-1/2
the fields
\chi^{a}
are canonically normalized. The self-coupling (
19
) is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{12}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}+\text{11 permutations}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{6}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}d{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c}}+T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c}b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}d{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}}+T^{b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}+T^{b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}d{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c}}\right].

(65)

In writing the latter we take into account that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(A)=\mathrm{Tr}(A^{T}),\quad(T^{a}\bar{T}^{b}T^{c}\bar{T}^{d})^{T}=(T^{d})^{*}(T^{c})^{T}(T^{b})^{*}(T^{a})^{T}=\bar{T}^{d}T^{c}\bar{T}^{b}T^{a},

so, e.g.,

\displaystyle T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}\equiv\mathrm{Tr}(T^{a}\bar{T}^{b}T^{c}\bar{T}^{d})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(\bar{T}^{d}T^{c}\bar{T}^{b}T^{a})=\mathrm{Tr}(T^{a}\bar{T}^{d}T^{c}\bar{T}^{b})\equiv T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}}.

The expressions for the RG functions are available in literature up to the five-loop level
11
11

11

In terms of
g_{i}=\lambda_{i}/6
.

Kalagov:2015gra
. We extend these results up to six loops. The one-loop contributions read

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{12}(n^{2}-n+8)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(n-1)+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{4},

(66)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{12}(2n-5),

(67)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv-\beta^{(1)}_{\tau}/\tau=-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{12}(n^{2}-n+2)-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{6}(n-1),

(68)

while two-loop corrections are

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{576}(n^{2}-n-2)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{144}(n-1)+\frac{\lambda^{2}_{2}}{1152}(n^{2}-3n+4),

(69)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}}{48}(3n-3n^{2}-14)-\frac{11\lambda^{2}_{1}\lambda_{2}}{36}(n-1)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda^{2}_{2}}{288}(15n-5n^{2}-92)-\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{24}(n-2),

(70)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{144}(5n-5n^{2}-82)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda^{2}_{2}}{36}(20-11n)+\frac{\lambda^{3}_{2}}{96}(7n-n^{2}-20),

(71)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{5\lambda_{1}^{2}}{288}(n^{2}-n-2)+\frac{5\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{72}(n-1)+\frac{5\lambda^{2}_{2}}{576}(n^{2}-3n+4).

(72)

All results at six loops are available online as supplementary material.

3.2.4
U(n)\times U(m)
model

Consider now eq. (
14
) with general complex
n\times m
matrix field
\phi=\{\phi_{\alpha i}\}
.
The model can be used to study phase transitions in massless QCD and five-loop RG functions are available in literature
Calabrese:2004uk
. We compute the six-loop contributions by means of decomposition (
15
) with
N_{a}=2nm
and

T^{a}
being complex
n\times m
matrices (c.f. eq. (
34
))

\displaystyle(T_{a})_{\alpha i}=\sqrt{T_{f}}\begin{cases}\delta_{\alpha,((a-1)~\text{div}~m)+1}\delta_{i,((a-1)~\text{mod}~m)+1},&a=1,\ldots mn\\
iT^{a-mn}_{\alpha i},&a=mn+1,\ldots 2mn,\end{cases}

(73)

satisfying

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}\bar{T}_{b})+\mathrm{Tr}(T_{b}\bar{T}_{a})=2T_{f}\delta^{ab},

and

\displaystyle T^{a}_{\alpha j}T^{a}_{\beta k}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{a}_{j\alpha}\bar{T}^{a}_{k\beta}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle T^{a}_{\alpha j}\bar{T}^{a}_{k\beta}

\displaystyle=2T_{f}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{jk}.

(74)

The calculations are carried out with
T_{f}=1/2
and the following representation of general self-coupling

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{12}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}}T^{c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}+\text{11 perms}\right]+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{12}\left[T^{a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d}}+\text{11 perms}\right],

(75)

where among all 24 permutations we exclude only those that correspond to the swapping between pairs of indices.

Our calculation employs eq. (
74
) and renders at one loop

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{6}(4+nm)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(n+m)+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{2},

(76)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{6}(n+m),

(77)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv-\beta^{(1)}_{\tau}/\tau=-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{6}(1+nm)-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{6}(n+m),

(78)

and at two loops

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{288}(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})(1+mn)+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{144}(n+m),

(79)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}}{24}(7+3mn)-\frac{11\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{36}(m+n)-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{72}(41+5mn)-\frac{\lambda^{2}_{3}}{12}(m+n),

(80)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{72}(41+5mn)-\frac{11\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{36}(m+n)-\frac{\lambda^{2}_{3}}{24}(5+mn),

(81)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{144}(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})(1+mn)+\frac{5\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{72}(n+m).

(82)

The full results are available as supplementary material.
It is worth noting that for
m=n
we get the four-loop results obtained in ref.
Steudtner:2020tzo
for the case of
U(n)\times U(n)
model.
12
12

12

In ref.
Steudtner:2020tzo
the RG functions are written in terms of
a_{u}\equiv hn\lambda_{2}/24
and
a_{v}\equiv hn^{2}\lambda_{1}/24
.

3.2.5
Field in the adjoint representation of
SU(n)

In recent ref.
Hnatic:2020kyo
a model
with
\phi
being hermitian matrix field in the adjoint representation of
SU(n)
is analyzed both with perturbative and non-perturbative methods.
In addition, the model was also considered as an example of application of the
ARGES
code
Steudtner:2021fzs
.
We generalize the Lagrangian of ref.
Hnatic:2020kyo
and include also a cubic term
13
13

13

The term breaks
Z_{2}
symmetry
\phi\to-\phi
imposed in ref.
Hnatic:2020kyo
.
(see also refs.
Ruegg:1980gf
;
Murphy:1983rf
;
Litim:2020jvl
) together with the vacuum energy (we rescale
f
and
\lambda_{2}
for convenience)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\phi(-\partial^{2}+m^{2})\phi\right]+\frac{\sqrt{n}f}{3!}\mathrm{Tr}\phi^{3}+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{4}\left(\mathrm{Tr}\phi^{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{n\lambda_{2}}{4!}\mathrm{Tr}\phi^{4}+\Lambda.

(83)

Obviously, we can easily treat the model
in our approach by means of the decomposition (
15
) with
T_{a}
being
SU(n)
generators. The latter satisfy the well-known relations
Cvitanovic:1976am

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(T_{a}T_{b})=T_{f}\delta_{ab},\quad T^{a}_{ij}T^{a}_{kl}=T_{f}\left(\delta_{il}\delta_{kl}-\frac{1}{n}\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}\right).

(84)

We utilize the normalization
T_{f}=1
and substitute

\displaystyle\lambda_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{3}\left[T^{ab}T^{cd}+T^{ac}T^{bd}+T^{ad}T^{bc}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.38945pt+\frac{n\lambda_{2}}{6}\left[T^{abcd}+T^{abdc}+T^{acbd}+T^{acdb}+T^{adbc}+T^{adcb}\right]

(85)

together with

\displaystyle h_{abc}=\frac{\sqrt{n}f}{2}\left[T^{abc}+T^{bac}\right].

(86)

We obtain the RG functions up to the six-loop level, and at one loop we have

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{3}(7+n^{2})+\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(4n^{2}-6)+\lambda_{2}^{2}(3+n^{2}),

(87)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{3}(2n^{2}-18),

(88)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{f}

\displaystyle=f\left[2\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}(n^{2}-6)\right],

(89)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{m^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{3}\left[\lambda_{1}(n^{2}+1)+\lambda_{2}(2n^{2}-3)\right]+\frac{f^{2}}{2}(n^{2}-4),

(90)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{4}}{2}(n^{2}-1).

(91)

The two-loop expressions are given by

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}}{36}(1+n^{2})-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{18}(3-2n^{2})+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{72}(18-6n^{2}+n^{4}),

(92)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}}{3}(11+3n^{2})+\frac{22\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}(3-2n^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda^{2}_{2}}{18}(306+42n^{2}+5n^{4})+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{3}(36+9n^{2}-2n^{4}),

(93)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}(77+5n^{2})+\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda^{2}_{2}}{9}(123-22n^{2})-\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{6}(174-16n^{2}+n^{4}),

(94)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{f}{24}\left[4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}(105-22n^{2})-2\lambda_{1}^{2}(35+3n^{2})-\lambda_{2}^{2}(630-138n^{2}+11n^{4})\right]

(95)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{m^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{5m^{2}}{36}\left[4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}(3-2n^{2})-2\lambda_{1}^{2}(1+n^{2})-\lambda_{2}^{2}(18-6n^{2}+n^{4})\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{2}}{12}\left[\lambda_{1}(36-5n^{2}-n^{4})-3\lambda_{2}(36-17n^{2}+2n^{4})\right],

(96)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=-\frac{m^{2}f^{2}}{4}(n^{2}-1)(n^{2}-4).

(97)

To compare our results with that of ref.
Hnatic:2020kyo
, one has to take into account that the latter correspond to
f=0
and are written in terms of
g_{i}/(8\pi^{2})
with
g_{1}=(n\lambda_{2})/6
and
g_{2}=\lambda_{1}/6
.
We also use the expressions obtained by means of
ARGES

Steudtner:2021fzs
to cross-check
\gamma_{\phi}
and the beta functions for
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
, and
m^{2}
up to 4 loops.

3.3
Higher rank
O(n)\times O(n)\times O(n)
tensor model

To give an example how to apply our general result to models with
more complicated index structure, we consider evaluation of the beta functions
in the model with
O(n)\times O(n)\times O(n)
symmetry

Giombi:2017dtl
.
The model Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{abc}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{abc}+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{4!}T^{\rm t}_{\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4}}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4!}T^{\rm p}_{\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4}}+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{4!}T^{\rm ds}_{\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4}},

(98)

where we follow the naming scheme from ref.
Giombi:2017dtl
for the interaction terms as “tetrahedral”,
“pillow” and “double-sum”. Again, we use fields as tensor indices of the structures
T^{i}
to indicate contractions of triplets of indexes with
\phi_{abc}
.
For convenience, we present the tensor structures in the following pictorial form:

}}\right)

(100)

To map the model onto our general result,
we associate open indices in general model (
1
) with multi index
i_{k}=\{a_{k},b_{k},c_{k}\}
, and rewrite the self-coupling (
98
) in the form:

\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}}=\lambda_{1}T^{\rm t}_{(i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4})}+\lambda_{2}T^{\rm p}_{(i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4})}+\lambda_{3}T^{\rm ds}_{(i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4})}

(101)

where
(i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4})
denotes symmetrization.
At one loop we get

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(1+n)+4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}+\frac{4\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9},

(102)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}^{2}(2+n)+\frac{4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}(2+n)+4\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9}(12+5n+n^{2}),

(103)

\displaystyle\beta^{(1)}_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}{3}+2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}n+\frac{2\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}{3}(1+n+n^{2})+\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9}(3+2n)+\frac{\lambda_{3}^{2}}{3}(8+n^{3}).

(104)

while two-loop contribution renders

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}}{18}(n^{3}-15n-10)-\frac{2\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}(n^{2}+4n+13)-\frac{10\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{3}}{3}n

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{54}(n^{3}+15n^{2}+93n+101)-\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}{9}(5n^{2}+17n+17)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}^{2}}{9}(5n^{3}+82)-\frac{2\lambda_{2}^{3}}{81}(2n^{2}+13n+24)-\frac{16\lambda_{2}^{2}\lambda_{3}}{9},

(105)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\lambda_{1}^{3}}{3}(n^{2}+n+4)-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{18}(n^{3}+12n^{2}+99n+98)-4\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{3}(n+2)

\displaystyle-\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9}(4n^{2}+18n+29)-\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}{3}(13n+16)-\frac{\lambda_{2}^{3}}{162}(5n^{3}+45n^{2}+243n+343)

\displaystyle-\frac{2\lambda_{2}^{2}\lambda_{3}}{9}(7n^{2}+15n+29)-\frac{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}^{2}}{9}(5n^{3}+82),

(106)

\displaystyle\beta^{(2)}_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{3}n}{3}-\frac{2\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}{9}(n^{2}+n+4)-\frac{5\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{3}}{18}(n^{3}+3n+2)-\frac{8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{2}}{9}(n+1)

\displaystyle-\frac{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}{9}(5n^{2}+5n+17)-\frac{22\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}^{2}}{3}n-\frac{7\lambda_{2}^{3}}{81}(n^{2}+3n+5)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{2}^{2}\lambda_{3}}{54}(5n^{3}+15n^{2}+93n+97)-\frac{22\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}^{2}}{9}(n^{2}+n+1)-\frac{\lambda_{3}^{3}}{3}(3n^{3}+14).

(107)

Modulo rescaling
\lambda_{i}=6g_{i}
, the obtained expressions coincide with those given in ref.
Giombi:2017dtl
.
Six-loop
14
14

14

Due to the large number of indices in
\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}}
(
101
), we use the JINR supercomputer “Govorun” to compute the required 571 six-loop tensors in parallel.
results can be found in the form of supplementary material.

3.4
Two-Higgs Doublet Model

Motivated by three-loop calculation
Bednyakov:2018cmx
in 2HDM model (see, e.g., refs.
Branco:2011iw
;
Ivanov:2017dad
for review),
we consider the following general renormalizable Higgs potential

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{2HDM}}

\displaystyle=m_{11}^{2}\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}+m_{22}^{2}\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}-\left(m_{12}^{2}\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}+\mathrm{h.c.}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{2}\left(\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)^{2}+\lambda_{3}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}\right)\left(\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)+\lambda_{4}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)\left(\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{5}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)^{2}+\lambda_{6}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{1}\right)\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)+\lambda_{7}\left(\Phi_{2}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)\left(\Phi_{1}^{\dagger}\Phi_{2}\right)+\mathrm{h.c.}\right],

(108)

where
\Phi_{1,2}
are
SU(2)
doublets. The self-couplings
\lambda_{1-4}
and the mass parameters
m_{11}^{2}
,
m_{22}^{2}
are real, while
\lambda_{5-7}
, and
m_{12}^{2}
can be complex.
Due to the freedom in redefinition of Higgs-field basis, only 11 of 14 real parameters in eq. (
108
) are independent.
In ref.
Bednyakov:2018cmx
convenient variables
Branco:2011iw
;
Ivanov:2017dad
and the so-called
reparametrization invariants
(see, e.g., ref.
Trautner:2018ipq
for a comprehensive study) were used to compute the RG functions.

In this work, we use another strategy and directly calculate the beta function of
\lambda_{1-7}
together with the anomalous dimensions of
m^{2}_{11}
,
m_{22}^{2}
, and
m^{2}_{12}
from our general expressions.
We enumerate all real components of two doublets
\Phi_{1,2}
and rewrite eq. (
108
) in the general form (
1
) with indices
a,b
, etc. running from one to eight.
We find full agreement with previous results and extend the latter up to six loops. We have checked that our expressions for
\beta_{\lambda_{2}}(\beta_{\lambda_{7}})
can be obtained from
\beta_{\lambda_{1}}(\beta_{\lambda_{6}})
via the replacement
\lambda_{1}\leftrightarrow\lambda_{2}
and
\lambda_{6}\leftrightarrow\lambda_{7}
.
One can use the same substitutions together with
m^{2}_{11}\leftrightarrow m^{2}_{22}
to get
\beta_{m_{22}^{2}}
from
\beta_{m_{11}^{2}}
.
We make the six-loop results available as supplementary material.

4
Conclusion

We considered the general renormalizable scalar QFT model and directly computed
the RG functions for the quartic and cubic self-couplings, mass parameter,
tadpole term, and vacuum energy. In deriving our results for dimensionless
quantities, we used the expressions for the
\mathrm{KR}^{\prime}
operation applied to individual
Feynman integrals. The latter are publicly available thanks to lengthy and
non-trivial calculations of ref.
Kompaniets:2017yct
. To compute the RG functions of
dimensionful parameters, we utilize the powerful dummy field technique.

To validate our general results, we considered several scalar models discussed
in the theory of critical phenomena. We found perfect agreement with known
results and extend them by computing several missing six-loop contributions.
Among the latter are the vacuum energy beta function in the
O(n)
model, the
anomalous dimensions of quadratic perturbations in the
O(n)\times O(m)
model,
and the self-coupling beta functions for
U(n)\times U(m)
, and
O(n)\times O(n)\times O(n)
models and the model with the Higgs field in the adjoint
representation of the
SU(n)
group. Additionally, we extend the three-loop
results for the general Two-Higgs-Doublet Model scalar sector to six loops.

We believe that the obtained state-of-the-art RG functions are of immediate
interest to the condensed-matter community. On the contrary, present six-loop
results can hardly find their applications in phenomenological analyses of the
Standard Model extensions in the near future. However, it is convenient to
estimate the influence of the high-order terms on extended Higgs sector studies,
which currently rely on the two- or three-loop RG.
Public codes for RG
analyses
Staub:2013tta
;
Sartore:2020gou
;
Litim:2020jvl
;
Deppisch:2020aoj
;
Thomsen:2021ncy

can be equipped with our results to carry out this kind of computations.

We also note that the expression for vacuum energy beta function is relevant for
effective potential
V_{\mathrm{eff}}(\phi)
RG improvement (see, e.g.,
ref.
Martin:2017lqn
). Moreover, in recent ref.
Manohar:2020nzp
,
the vacuum energy function’s role is emphasized in the effective field theory
approach to
V_{\mathrm{eff}}(\phi)
computation in models with many different
scales.

Let us also mention here that seven-loop results
Schnetz:2016fhy
can not be directly used in our approach. We expect that in the future when the corresponding diagram-by-diagram counter-terms will be available, one can almost immediately extend our general expressions to one more loop. However, our experience tells us that the calculation of tensor structures in specific models can be very time-consuming.

refs.
Steudtner:2020tzo
;
Steudtner:2021fzs
and sharing his four-loop
results. We are grateful to the Joint Institute for Nuclear Research for letting
us use their supercomputer “Govorun”. The work of A.B. is supported by the
Grant of the Russian Federation Government, Agreement No. 14.W03.31.0026 from
15.02.2018. The work of A.P. is supported by the Foundation for the Advancement
of Theoretical Physics and Mathematics “BASIS.”

Appendix A
Deriving dimensionful couplings RG with Dummy field method

The author of ref.
Steudtner:2020tzo
introduced a convenient representation for four-loop quartic-coupling beta function with all the self-couplings involving external indices explicitly factorized.
We adopt this ansatz to all loops

\displaystyle\beta_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\left[\lambda_{abcf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fd}+\lambda_{abdf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fc}+\lambda_{acdf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fb}+\lambda_{bcdf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fa}\right]+\left[\lambda_{abef}\lambda_{cdgh}\bigcirc_{ef|gh}+5~\text{perm.}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\lambda_{abef}\lambda_{cghi}\lambda_{djkl}\bigtriangleup_{ef|ghi|jkl}+11~\text{perm.}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\lambda_{aefg}\lambda_{bhij}\lambda_{cklm}\lambda_{dnop}\square_{efg|hij|klm|nop}+23~\text{perm.}\right]

(109)

and re-derive the RG functions for the dimensionful parameters entering the Lagrangian (
1
).
In eq. (
109
) “perm.” denotes the terms, which can be obtained from the respective expressions via non-equivalent permutations of external indices. It is convenient to represent eq. (
109
) in the following pictorial form:

\displaystyle\beta_{abcd}

(110)

where external self-couplings are denoted by blue vertices (see figure
1
) and we
use the notation given in figure
2
for the non-external parts of four-point functions.
It is worth noting that
\bigcirc_{ab|cd}
,
\bigtriangleup_{ab|cde|fgh}
, and
\square_{abc|def|ghi|jkl}
do not need to be symmetric w.r.t. permutations of (group of) indices.
Each group of indices is contracted with symmetric couplings, and, thus,
does not need to be explicitly symmetrized. However, we explicitly take into account that an external index
a,b,c
, or
d
can be attached to any group via a quartic vertex (this corresponds the permutations indicated, e.g., in eq. (
110
)).
Due to this, we distinguish index groups and mark them by numbers (c.f., figure
2
).

(111)

Here the trilinear couplings correspond to red vertices (see figure
1
) and again we have to explicitly take into account
permutations of external indices. The analytic expression is given by

\displaystyle\beta_{abc}

\displaystyle=\left[h_{abf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fc}+h_{acf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fb}+h_{bcf}\gamma^{\phi}_{fa}\right]+\left[\lambda_{abef}h_{cgh}\left(\bigcirc_{ef|gh}+\bigcirc_{gh|ef}\right)+2~\text{perm.}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\lambda_{abef}\lambda_{cghi}h_{jkl}\left(\bigtriangleup_{ef|ghi|jkl}+\bigtriangleup_{ef|jkl|ghi}\right)+2~\text{perm.}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[h_{aef}\lambda_{bghi}\lambda_{cjkl}\bigtriangleup_{ef|ghi|jkl}+5~\text{perm.}\right]+\left[\lambda_{aefg}\lambda_{bhij}\lambda_{cklm}h_{nop}\left(\square_{efg|hij|klm|nop}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\square_{nop|efg|hij|klm}+\square_{klm|nop|efg|hij}+\square_{hij|klm|nop|efg}\right)+5~\text{perm.}\right]

(112)

To obtain the beta function for mass parameter we contract eq. (
111
) with one more dummy field
x_{c}
.
Dividing the result by the factor of two, we get

\displaystyle\beta_{ab}

\displaystyle=\left[\hbox to104.03pt{\vbox to74.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.9921pt\lower-37.07526pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{

(115)

where the orange vertex corresponds the tadpole parameter
t_{a}
of the Lagrangian (
1
). The analytic form of eq. (
115
) looks like

\displaystyle\beta_{a}

\displaystyle=t_{f}\gamma^{\phi}_{fb}+h_{aef}m^{2}_{gh}\left[\bigcirc_{ef|gh}+\bigcirc_{gh|ef}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[h_{aef}h_{ghi}h_{jkl}\bigtriangleup_{ef|ghi|jkl}+m^{2}_{ef}\lambda_{aghi}h_{jkl}\left(\bigtriangleup_{ef|ghi|jkl}+\bigtriangleup_{ef|jkl|ghi}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\lambda_{aefg}h_{hij}h_{klm}h_{nop}\left[\square_{efg|hij|klm|nop}+\square_{nop|efg|hij|klm}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\square_{klm|nop|efg|hij}+\square_{hij|klm|nop|efg}\right].

(116)

x_{a}

\displaystyle\beta_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=m^{2}_{ef}m^{2}_{gh}\bigcirc_{ef|gh}+m^{2}_{ef}h_{ghi}h_{jkl}\bigtriangleup_{ef|ghi|jkl}+h_{efg}h_{hij}h_{klm}h_{nop}\square_{efg|hij|klm|nop}.

Loop expansion of the structures

\displaystyle\bigcirc_{ab|cd}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=1}^{6}\bigcirc^{(l)}_{ab|cd},

(119)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=1}^{6}\bigtriangleup^{(l)}_{ab|cde|fgh},

(120)

\displaystyle\square_{abc|def|ghi|jkl}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=1}^{6}\square^{(l)}_{abc|def|ghi|jkl}.
